\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}\leq\frac{c_{0}\rho}{32\cdot 36\cdot\sqrt{d\log\frac{8S}{\epsilon}}}=\mathcal{O}(\rho d^{-1/2})\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\epsilon_{\mathrm{energy}}\leq\frac{c_{0}\rho\sqrt{2\tau}}{32\cdot 5}=\mathcal{O}(\rho\tau^{1/2}),

then for any
\lambda
-warm start with respect to
\mu_{*}
the output of both standard and projected implementation of Alg.
2
satisfies

\mathrm{TV}\left(\mu_{S},\mu_{*}\right)=\frac{\epsilon}{2}+\lambda\left(1-\frac{c_{0}^{2}\rho^{2}}{4}\cdot\tau\right)^{S}+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(d^{1/2}\rho^{-1}\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}})+\mathcal{O}({\rho^{-1}\tau^{-1/2}\epsilon_{\mathrm{energy}}})

Proof.

We characterize the condition on the step size
\tau
.
Combining Lemma
C.2
and Corollary
C.11
, it requires the range of
\tau
to satisfy

\tau\leq{16^{-1}\cdot(3LR+\left\|\nabla g({\bm{0}})\right\|+\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}})^{-2}}.

Under this condition, we have

\tau\leq\frac{d\log\frac{8S}{\epsilon}}{(3LR+G)^{2}},\quad\tau\leq\frac{d\log\frac{8S}{\epsilon}}{\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}^{2}},\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\tau\leq\left(72^{2}\cdot\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}^{2}\cdot d\log\frac{8S}{\epsilon}\right)^{-1}

which implies

(3LR+G)\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}\cdot\tau\leq\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}\sqrt{\tau}\cdot\sqrt{d\log\frac{8S}{\epsilon}}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}^{2}\cdot\tau\leq\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}\sqrt{\tau}\cdot\sqrt{d\log\frac{8S}{\epsilon}}.

Then, we have

\displaystyle\delta=

\displaystyle 16\cdot\left[\frac{3\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}}{2}\cdot\sqrt{\tau d\log\frac{8S}{\epsilon}}+\frac{(3LR+G)\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}\cdot\tau}{2}+\frac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}^{2}\cdot\tau}{4}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 16\cdot\left[\frac{3\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}}{2}\cdot\sqrt{\tau d\log\frac{8S}{\epsilon}}+\frac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}}{2}\cdot\sqrt{\tau d\log\frac{8S}{\epsilon}}+\frac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}}{4}\cdot\sqrt{\tau d\log\frac{8S}{\epsilon}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 36\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}\cdot\sqrt{\tau d\log\frac{8S}{\epsilon}}\leq\frac{1}{2}

which matches the requirement of Lemma
C.3
.
Under this condition, if we require

\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}\leq\frac{c_{0}\rho}{32\cdot 36\cdot\sqrt{d\log\frac{8S}{\epsilon}}}=\mathcal{O}(\rho d^{-1/2})\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\epsilon_{\mathrm{energy}}\leq\frac{c_{0}\rho\sqrt{2\tau}}{32\cdot 5}=\mathcal{O}(\rho\tau^{1/2}),

it makes

\delta+5\epsilon_{\mathrm{energy}}\leq 36\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}\cdot\sqrt{\tau d\log\frac{8S}{\epsilon}}+5\epsilon_{\mathrm{energy}}\leq\frac{c_{0}\rho\sqrt{2\tau}}{16}\leq\frac{\phi}{16}

and satisfies the requirements shown in Lemma
C.12
.

Then, we are able to put the results of these lemmas together to establish the convergence of Alg.
2
.
Note that if
\mu_{0}
is a
\lambda
-warm start to
\mu_{*}
, it must be a
\lambda
-warm start to
\tilde{\mu}_{*}
since
\tilde{\mu}_{*}({\mathcal{A}})\geq\mu_{*}({\mathcal{A}})
for all
{\mathcal{A}}\in\Omega
.
Combining Lemma
C.2
, Lemma
C.12
and Lemma
C.13
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}\left(\mu_{S},\mu_{*}\right)\leq

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}\left(\mu_{S},\tilde{\mu}_{S}\right)+\mathrm{TV}\left(\tilde{\mu}_{S},\tilde{\mu}_{*}\right)+\mathrm{TV}\left(\tilde{\mu}_{*},\mu_{*}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon}{4}+\left(\lambda\cdot\left(1-\frac{\phi^{2}}{8}\right)^{S}+\frac{16(\delta+5\epsilon_{\mathrm{energy}})}{\phi}\right)+\frac{\epsilon}{4}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon}{2}+\lambda\left(1-\frac{c_{0}^{2}\rho^{2}}{4}\cdot\tau\right)^{S}+408\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{d\log\frac{8S}{\epsilon}}}{c_{0}\rho}+\frac{57\epsilon_{\mathrm{energy}}}{c_{0}\rho\sqrt{\tau}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon}{2}+\lambda\left(1-\frac{c_{0}^{2}\rho^{2}}{4}\cdot\tau\right)^{S}+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(d^{1/2}\rho^{-1}\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}})+\mathcal{O}({\rho^{-1}\tau^{-1/2}\epsilon_{\mathrm{energy}}}).

After combining this result with the choice of parameters shown in Lemma
C.14
, the proof is completed.
∎

Lemma C.16
.

Under the same assumptions and hyperparameter settings made in Theorem
C.15
, we use Gaussian-type initialization

\frac{\mu_{0}(\mathrm{d}{\bm{z}})}{\mathrm{d}{\bm{z}}}\propto\exp\left(-L\|{\bm{z}}\|^{2}-\frac{\left\|{\bm{x}}_{0}-e^{-\eta}{\bm{z}}\right\|^{2}}{2(1-e^{-2\eta})}\right).

If we set the iteration number as

S=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(L\rho^{-2}\cdot\left(d+m_{2}^{2}\right)\tau^{-1}\right),

the standard and projected implementation of Alg.
2
can achieve

\mathrm{TV}\left(\mu_{S},\mu_{*}\right)\leq\frac{3\epsilon}{4}+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(d^{1/2}\rho^{-1}\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}})+\mathcal{O}({\rho^{-1}\tau^{-1/2}\epsilon_{\mathrm{energy}}}).

Proof.

We reformulate the target distribution
\mu_{*}
and the initial distribution
\mu_{0}
as follows

\displaystyle\frac{\mu_{*}(\mathrm{d}{\bm{z}})}{\mathrm{d}{\bm{z}}}\propto\exp\left[-\left(f_{(K-k-1)\eta}({\bm{z}})+\frac{3L\|{\bm{z}}\|^{2}}{2}\right)-\left(\frac{\left\|{\bm{x}}_{0}-e^{-\eta}{\bm{z}}\right\|^{2}}{2(1-e^{-2\eta})}-\frac{3L\|{\bm{z}}\|^{2}}{2}\right)\right]\coloneqq\exp\left(-\phi({\bm{z}})-\psi({\bm{z}})\right),

\displaystyle\frac{\mu_{0}(\mathrm{d}{\bm{z}})}{{\bm{z}}}\propto\exp\left[-L\|{\bm{z}}\|^{2}-\frac{3L\|{\bm{z}}\|^{2}}{2}-\left(\frac{\left\|{\bm{x}}_{0}-e^{-\eta}{\bm{z}}\right\|^{2}}{2(1-e^{-2\eta})}-\frac{3L\|{\bm{z}}\|^{2}}{2}\right)\right]=\exp\left[-\frac{5L\|{\bm{z}}\|^{2}}{2}-\psi({\bm{z}})\right].

Under this condition, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mu_{0}(\mathrm{d}{\bm{z}})}{\mu_{*}(\mathrm{d}{\bm{z}})}\leq\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp\left(-\phi({\bm{z}}^{\prime})-\psi({\bm{z}}^{\prime})\right)\mathrm{d}{\bm{z}}^{\prime}}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp\left(-5L/2\cdot\|{\bm{z}}^{\prime}\|^{2}-\psi({\bm{z}}^{\prime})\right)\mathrm{d}{\bm{z}}^{\prime}}\cdot\exp\left(\phi({\bm{z}})-\frac{5L\|{\bm{z}}\|^{2}}{2}\right)

(74)

Due to Assumption
[A1]
, we have

\frac{L{\bm{I}}}{2}\preceq\nabla^{2}f_{(K-k-1)\eta}({\bm{z}}^{\prime})+\frac{3L}{2}=\nabla^{2}\phi({\bm{z}}^{\prime})\preceq\frac{5L{\bm{I}}}{2},

which means

\displaystyle\phi({\bm{z}})\leq\phi({\bm{z}}_{*})+\frac{5L}{4}\cdot\left\|{\bm{z}}-{\bm{z}}_{*}\right\|^{2}\leq\phi({\bm{z}}_{*})+\frac{5L\|{\bm{z}}\|^{2}}{2}+\frac{5L\|{\bm{z}}_{*}\|^{2}}{2}

and

\exp\left(\phi({\bm{z}})-\frac{5L\|{\bm{z}}\|^{2}}{2}\right)\leq\exp\left(\phi({\bm{z}}_{*})+\frac{5L\|{\bm{z}}_{*}\|^{2}}{2}\right).

(75)

Since the function
\phi({\bm{z}})
is strongly log-concave, it satisfies

\nabla\phi({\bm{z}})\cdot{\bm{z}}\geq\frac{L\|{\bm{z}}\|^{2}}{4}-\frac{\|\nabla\phi({\bm{0}})\|}{L}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\phi({\bm{z}})\geq\frac{L\|{\bm{z}}\|^{2}}{16}+\phi({\bm{z}}_{*})-\frac{\|\nabla\phi({\bm{0}})\|^{2}}{2L}

due to Lemma
E.3
and Lemma
E.4
.
Under these conditions, we have

\displaystyle\int\exp\left[-\phi({\bm{z}}^{\prime})-\psi({\bm{z}}^{\prime})\right]\mathrm{d}{\bm{z}}^{\prime}\leq\exp\left(-\phi({\bm{z}}_{*})+\frac{\|\nabla\phi({\bm{0}})\|^{2}}{2L}\right)\cdot\int\exp\left[-\frac{L\|{\bm{z}}^{\prime}\|^{2}}{16}-\psi({\bm{z}}^{\prime})\right]\mathrm{d}{\bm{z}}^{\prime}

(76)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\phi({\bm{z}}_{*})+\frac{\|\nabla\phi({\bm{0}})\|^{2}}{2L}\right)\cdot\int\exp\left[-\frac{23L\|{\bm{z}}^{\prime}\|^{2}}{16}-\frac{\left\|{\bm{x}}_{0}-e^{-\eta}{\bm{z}}^{\prime}\right\|^{2}}{2(1-e^{-2\eta})}\right]\mathrm{d}{\bm{z}}^{\prime}

Besides, we have

\displaystyle\int\exp\left[-\frac{5L\|{\bm{z}}^{\prime}\|^{2}}{2}-\psi({\bm{z}}^{\prime})\right]\mathrm{d}{\bm{z}}^{\prime}=\int\exp\left[-L\|{\bm{z}}^{\prime}\|^{2}-\frac{\left\|{\bm{x}}_{0}-e^{-\eta}{\bm{z}}^{\prime}\right\|^{2}}{2(1-e^{-2\eta})}\right]\mathrm{d}{\bm{z}}^{\prime},

which implies

\displaystyle\int\exp\left[-\frac{5L\|{\bm{z}}^{\prime}\|^{2}}{2}-\psi({\bm{z}}^{\prime})\right]\mathrm{d}{\bm{z}}^{\prime}\cdot\int\exp\left[-\frac{7L\|{\bm{z}}^{\prime}\|^{2}}{16}\right]\mathrm{d}{\bm{z}}^{\prime}

(77)

\displaystyle\geq\int\exp\left[-\frac{23L\|{\bm{z}}^{\prime}\|^{2}}{16}-\frac{\left\|{\bm{x}}_{0}-e^{-\eta}{\bm{z}}^{\prime}\right\|^{2}}{2(1-e^{-2\eta})}\right]\mathrm{d}{\bm{z}}^{\prime}

Plugging Eq.
75
, Eq.
76
and Eq.
77
into Eq.
74
, we have

\frac{\mu_{0}(\mathrm{d}{\bm{z}})}{\tilde{\mu}_{*}(\mathrm{d}{\bm{z}})}\leq\exp\left(\frac{5L\|{\bm{z}}_{*}\|^{2}}{2}+\frac{\|\nabla\phi({\bm{0}})\|^{2}}{2L}\right)\cdot\int\exp\left[-\frac{7L\|{\bm{z}}^{\prime}\|^{2}}{16}\right]\mathrm{d}{\bm{z}}^{\prime}.

(78)

Due to the strong convexity of
\phi
, it has

\left\|{\bm{z}}_{*}\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{4\|\nabla\phi({\bm{0}})-\nabla\phi({\bm{z}}_{*})\|^{2}}{L^{2}}=\frac{4\|\nabla\phi({\bm{0}})\|^{2}}{L^{2}}

and

\left\|\nabla\phi({\bm{0}})\right\|^{2}=\left\|\nabla f_{(K-k-1)\eta}({\bm{0}})\right\|^{2}\leq 2L^{2}(d+m_{2}^{2})

where the inequality follows from Eq.
72
.
Combining with the fact

\int\exp\left[-\frac{7L\|{\bm{z}}^{\prime}\|^{2}}{16}\right]\mathrm{d}{\bm{z}}^{\prime}=\left(\frac{16\pi}{7L}\right)^{d/2},

Eq.
78
can be relaxed to

\lambda\leq\max_{{\bm{z}}}\frac{\mu_{0}(\mathrm{d}{\bm{z}})}{\tilde{\mu}_{*}(\mathrm{d}{\bm{z}})}\leq\exp\left(22L\cdot(d+m_{2}^{2})\right)\cdot\left(\frac{16\pi}{L}\right)^{d/2}=\exp\left(\mathcal{O}(L(d+m_{2}^{2}))\right)

which is independent on
\|{\bm{x}}_{0}\|
.
Then, In order to ensure the convergence of the total variation distance is smaller than
\epsilon
, it suffices to choose
\tau
and
S
such that

\lambda\left(1-\frac{c_{0}^{2}\rho^{2}}{4}\cdot\tau\right)^{S}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{4}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad S=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log(\lambda/\epsilon)}{\rho^{2}\tau}\right)=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(L\rho^{-2}\cdot\left(d+m_{2}^{2}\right)\tau^{-1}\right),

where the last two inequalities follow from Theorem
C.15
.
Hence, the proof is completed.
∎

Theorem C.17
.

Under Assumption
[A1]
–
[A2]
, for Alg.
1
, we choose

\eta=\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{2L+1}{2L}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad K=4L\cdot\log\frac{(1+L^{2})d+\left\|\nabla f_{*}({\bm{0}})\right\|^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}

and implement Step 3 of Alg.
1
with projected Alg.
2
.
For the
k
-th run of Alg.
2
, we use Gaussian-type initialization

\frac{\mu_{0}(\mathrm{d}{\bm{z}})}{\mathrm{d}{\bm{z}}}\propto\exp\left(-L\|{\bm{z}}\|^{2}-\frac{\left\|\hat{{\bm{x}}}_{k}-e^{-\eta}{\bm{z}}\right\|^{2}}{2(1-e^{-2\eta})}\right).

If we set the hyperparameters as shown in Lemma
C.16
, it can achieve

\mathrm{TV}\left(\hat{p}_{K\eta},p_{*}\right)\leq\epsilon+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(Ld^{1/2}\rho^{-1}\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}})+\mathcal{O}(\hat{\tau}^{-1/2}\cdot L^{2}(d^{1/2}+m_{2}+Z){\rho^{-1}\epsilon_{\mathrm{energy}}})

with a gradient complexity as follows

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(L^{4}\rho^{-2}\hat{\tau}^{-1}\cdot\left(d+m_{2}^{2}\right)^{2}Z^{2}\right)

for any
\hat{\tau}\in(0,1)
where
Z
denotes the maximal
l_{2}
norm of particles appearing in outer loops (Alg.
1
).

Proof.

According to Lemma
B.3
, we know that under the choice

\eta=\frac{1}{2}\ln\frac{2L+1}{2L},

it requires to run Alg.
2
for
K
times where

K=4L\cdot\log\frac{(1+L^{2})d+\left\|\nabla f_{*}({\bm{0}})\right\|^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}.

For each run of Alg.
2
, we require the total variation error to achieve

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}\left(\hat{p}_{(k+1)\eta|k\eta}(\cdot|\hat{{\bm{x}}}),p^{\leftarrow}_{(k+1)\eta|k\eta}(\cdot|\hat{{\bm{x}}})\right)\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon}{4L}\cdot\left[\log\frac{(1+L^{2})d+\left\|\nabla f_{*}({\bm{0}})\right\|^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}\right]^{-1}

\displaystyle+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(d^{1/2}\rho^{-1}\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}})+\mathcal{O}({\rho^{-1}\tau_{k}^{-1/2}\epsilon_{\mathrm{energy}}}).

Combining with Lemma
C.16
, we consider a step size

\displaystyle\tau_{k}=

\displaystyle C_{\tau}\cdot\left(L^{2}\left(d+m_{2}^{2}+\|\hat{{\bm{x}}}_{k}\|^{2}\right)\cdot\log\frac{48LS\log\frac{(1+L^{2})d+\left\|\nabla f_{*}({\bm{0}})\right\|^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}}{\epsilon}\right)^{-1}\cdot\hat{\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(L^{-2}\cdot(d+m_{2}^{2}+\|\hat{{\bm{x}}}_{k}\|^{2})^{-1}\cdot\hat{\tau})

where
\tau^{\prime}\in(0,1)
, to solve the
k
-th inner sampling subproblem.
Then, the maximum iteration number will be

S=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(L^{3}\rho^{-2}\hat{\tau}^{-1}\cdot\left(d+m_{2}^{2}\right)^{2}\cdot\|\hat{{\bm{x}}}_{k}\|^{2}\right).

This means that with the total gradient complexity

\displaystyle K\cdot S=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(L^{4}\rho^{-2}\hat{\tau}^{-1}\cdot\left(d+m_{2}^{2}\right)^{2}Z^{2}\right)

where
Z
denotes the maximal
l_{2}
norm of particles appearing in outer loops (Alg.
1
),
we can obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}\left(\hat{p}_{K\eta},p_{*}\right)\leq

\displaystyle\epsilon+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(Kd^{1/2}\rho^{-1}\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}})+\mathcal{O}(KL(d+m_{2}^{2}+Z^{2})^{1/2}\hat{\tau}^{-1/2}{\rho^{-1}\epsilon_{\mathrm{energy}}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\epsilon+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(Ld^{1/2}\rho^{-1}\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}})+\mathcal{O}(\hat{\tau}^{-1/2}\cdot L^{2}(d^{1/2}+m_{2}+Z){\rho^{-1}\epsilon_{\mathrm{energy}}}).

Hence, the proof is completed.
∎

Lemma C.18
.

Suppose we implement Alg.
2
with its projected version, we have

Z^{2}\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(L^{3}(d+m_{2}^{2})^{2}\rho^{-2}\right).

where
Z
denotes the maximal
l_{2}
norm of particles appearing in outer loops (Alg.
1
)

Proof.

Suppose we implement Alg.
2
with its projected version, where each update will be projected to a ball with a ratio
r
shown in Lemma
C.2
.
Under these conditions, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{{\bm{x}}}_{K}\right\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{{\bm{x}}}_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{K}(\hat{{\bm{x}}}_{i}-\hat{{\bm{x}}}_{i-1})\right\|^{2}\leq(K+1)\left\|\hat{{\bm{x}}}_{0}\right\|^{2}+(K+1)\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{K}\left\|\hat{{\bm{x}}}_{i}-\hat{{\bm{x}}}_{i-1}\right\|^{2}

For each
i\in\{1,2,\ldots K\}
, we have

\left\|\hat{{\bm{x}}}_{i}-\hat{{\bm{x}}}_{i-1}\right\|^{2}=\left\|{\bm{z}}_{S}-{\bm{z}}_{0}\right\|^{2}\leq(S+1)\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{S}\left\|{\bm{z}}_{j}-{\bm{z}}_{j-1}\right\|^{2}\leq 2S\cdot r^{2}.

Follows from Lemma
C.16
, it has

S\cdot r^{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log(\lambda/\epsilon)}{\rho^{2}\tau}\right)\cdot\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\tau d\right)=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{d\log(\lambda/\epsilon)}{\rho^{2}}\right)=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(L(d+m_{2}^{2})^{2}\rho^{-2}\right).

Then, we have

Z^{2}\leq\mathcal{O}(K^{2})\cdot\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(L(d+m_{2}^{2})^{2}\rho^{-2}\right)=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(L^{3}(d+m_{2}^{2})^{2}\rho^{-2}\right),

Hence, the proof is completed.
∎

C.5
Control the error from Energy Estimation

Corollary C.19
.

Suppose the diffusion model
{\bm{s}}_{{\bm{\theta}}}
satisfies

\left\|\hat{{\bm{s}}}_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\bm{x}},t)+\nabla\log p_{t}({\bm{x}})\right\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{\rho\epsilon}{Ld^{1/2}},

and another parameterized model
\hat{l}_{\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}}({\bm{x}},t)
is used to estimate the log-likelihood of
p_{t}({\bm{x}})
satisfying

\left\|\hat{l}_{{\bm{\theta}}^{\prime}}({\bm{x}},t)+\log p_{t}({\bm{x}})\right\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{\rho\epsilon}{L^{2}\cdot(d^{1/2}+m_{2}+Z)}.

If we implement Alg.
1
with the projected version of Alg.
2
, it has

\mathrm{TV}\left(\hat{p}_{K\eta},p_{*}\right)\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)

with the following gradient complexity

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(L^{4}\rho^{-2}\cdot\left(d+m_{2}^{2}\right)^{2}Z^{2}\right).

Proof.

Since we have highly accurate scores and energy estimation, we can construct
{\bm{s}}_{{\bm{\theta}}}
and
r_{{\bm{\theta}}^{\prime}}
(shown in Eq.
22
) for the
k
-th inner loop as follows

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\bm{s}}_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\bm{z}})=\hat{{\bm{s}}}_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\bm{z}},(K-k-1)\eta)+\frac{e^{-2\eta}{\bm{z}}-e^{-\eta}\hat{{\bm{x}}}_{k}}{1-e^{-2\eta}}

\displaystyle r_{{\bm{\theta}}^{\prime}}({\bm{z}},{\bm{z}}^{\prime})=\hat{l}_{{\bm{\theta}}^{\prime}}({\bm{z}},(K-k-1)\eta)+\frac{\left\|\hat{{\bm{x}}}_{k}-e^{-\eta}\cdot{\bm{z}}\right\|^{2}}{2(1-e^{-2\eta})}

\displaystyle-\left(\hat{l}_{{\bm{\theta}}^{\prime}}({\bm{z}}^{\prime},(K-k-1)\eta)+\frac{\left\|\hat{{\bm{x}}}_{k}-e^{-\eta}\cdot{\bm{z}}^{\prime}\right\|^{2}}{2(1-e^{-2\eta})}\right).

Under these conditions, we have

\epsilon_{\mathrm{energy}}\leq\frac{\rho\epsilon}{L^{2}\cdot(d^{1/2}+m_{2}+Z)}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}\leq\frac{\rho\epsilon}{Ld^{1/2}}.

Plugging these results into Theorem
C.17
and setting
\hat{\tau}=1/2
, we have

\mathrm{TV}\left(\hat{p}_{K\eta},p_{*}\right)\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)

with the following gradient complexity

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(L^{4}\rho^{-2}\cdot\left(d+m_{2}^{2}\right)^{2}Z^{2}\right).

∎

Corollary C.20
.

Suppose the score estimation is extremely small, i.e.,

\left\|\hat{{\bm{s}}}_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\bm{x}},t)+\nabla\log p_{t}({\bm{x}})\right\|_{\infty}\ll\frac{\rho\epsilon}{Ld^{1/2}},

and the log-likelihood function of
p_{t}
has a bounded
3
-order derivative, e.g.,

\left\|\nabla^{(3)}f({\bm{z}})\right\|\leq L,

we have a non-parametric estimation for log-likelihood to make we have
\mathrm{TV}\left(\hat{p}_{K\eta},p_{*}\right)\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
with

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(L^{4}\rho^{-3}\cdot\left(d+m_{2}^{2}\right)^{2}Z^{3}\cdot\epsilon\right).

gradient calls.

Proof.

Combining the Alg.
2
and the definition of
\epsilon_{\mathrm{energy}}
shown in Lemma
C.4
, we actually require to control

\epsilon_{\mathrm{energy}}\coloneqq\left(g(\tilde{{\bm{z}}}_{s})-g({\bm{z}}_{s})\right)-r_{\theta}(\tilde{{\bm{z}}}_{s},{\bm{z}}_{s})

for any
s\in[0,S-1]
.
Then, we start to construct
r_{\theta}(\tilde{{\bm{z}}}_{s},{\bm{z}}_{s})
.
Since we have

g(\tilde{{\bm{z}}}_{s})-g({\bm{z}}_{s})=f_{(K-k-1)\eta}(\tilde{{\bm{z}}}_{s})+\frac{\left\|{\bm{x}}_{0}-\tilde{{\bm{z}}}_{s}\cdot e^{-\eta}\right\|^{2}}{2(1-e^{-2\eta})}-f_{(K-k-1)\eta}({{\bm{z}}}_{s})-\frac{\left\|{\bm{x}}_{0}-{{\bm{z}}}_{s}\cdot e^{-\eta}\right\|^{2}}{2(1-e^{-2\eta})},

we should only estimate the difference of the energy function
f_{(K-k-1)\eta}
which will be presented as
f
for abbreviation.
Besides, we define the following function

h(t)=f\left(\left(\tilde{{\bm{z}}}_{s}-{\bm{z}}_{s}\right)\cdot t+{\bm{z}}_{s}\right),

which means

\displaystyle h^{(1)}(t)\coloneqq\frac{\mathrm{d}h(t)}{\mathrm{d}t}=\nabla f\left(\left(\tilde{{\bm{z}}}_{s}-{\bm{z}}_{s}\right)\cdot t+{\bm{z}}_{s}\right)\cdot\left(\tilde{{\bm{z}}}_{s}-{\bm{z}}_{s}\right)

\displaystyle h^{(2)}(t)\coloneqq\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}h(t)}{(\mathrm{d}t)^{2}}=\left(\tilde{{\bm{z}}}_{s}-{\bm{z}}_{s}\right)^{\top}\nabla^{2}f\left(\left(\tilde{{\bm{z}}}_{s}-{\bm{z}}_{s}\right)\cdot t+{\bm{z}}_{s}\right)\left(\tilde{{\bm{z}}}_{s}-{\bm{z}}_{s}\right)

Under the high-order smoothness condition, i.e.,

\left\|\nabla^{3}f({\bm{z}})\right\|\leq L

where
\|\cdot\|
denotes the nuclear norm,
then we have

\left|h(1)-h(0)\right|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{2}\frac{h^{(i)}(0)}{i!}+\frac{L\cdot\left\|\tilde{{\bm{z}}}_{s}-{\bm{z}}_{s}\right\|^{3}}{3!}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{2}\frac{h^{(i)}(0)}{i!}+\frac{Lr^{3}}{3!}.

It means we need to approximate
h^{(i)}
with high accuracy.

For
i=1
, the ground truth
h^{(1)}(0)
is

h^{(1)}(0)=\frac{\mathrm{d}h(t)}{\mathrm{d}t}=\nabla f\left({\bm{z}}_{s}\right)\cdot\left(\tilde{{\bm{z}}}_{s}-{\bm{z}}_{s}\right)

we can approximate it numerically as

\tilde{h}^{(1)}(0)\coloneqq s_{\theta}({\bm{z}}_{s})\cdot\left(\tilde{{\bm{z}}}_{s}-{\bm{z}}_{s}\right)

since we have score approximation.
Then it has

\delta^{(1)}(0)=h^{(1)}(0)-\tilde{h}^{(1)}(0)\leq\left\|\nabla f({\bm{z}}_{s})-s_{\theta}({\bm{z}}_{s})\right\|\cdot\left\|\tilde{{\bm{z}}}_{s}-{\bm{z}}_{s}\right\|\leq\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}\cdot r.

(79)

Then, for
i=2
, we obtain the ground truth
h^{(2)}(0)
by

h^{(1)}(t)-h^{(1)}(0)=\int_{0}^{t}h^{(2)}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau=th^{(2)}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}h^{(2)}(\tau)-h^{(2)}(0)\mathrm{d}\tau,

which means

h^{(2)}(0)=\frac{h^{(1)}(t)-h^{(1)}(0)}{t}+\frac{1}{t}\cdot\int_{0}^{t}h^{(2)}(\tau)-h^{(2)}(0)\mathrm{d}\tau.

If we use the differential to approximate
h^{(2)}(0)
, i.e.,

\tilde{h}^{(2)}(0)\coloneqq\frac{\tilde{h}^{(1)}(t)-\tilde{h}^{(1)}(0)}{t},

we find the error term will be

\delta^{(2)}(0)=\left|h^{(2)}(0)-\tilde{h}^{(2)}(0)\right|=\left|\frac{2\delta^{(1)}}{t}+\frac{1}{t}\cdot\int_{0}^{t}h^{(2)}(\tau)-h^{(2)}(0)\mathrm{d}\tau\right|.

(80)

If we use smoothness to relax the integration term, we have

\left|h^{(2)}(\tau)-h^{(2)}(0)\right|\leq\left\|\nabla^{2}f\left(\left(\tilde{{\bm{z}}}_{s}-{\bm{z}}_{s}\right)\cdot\tau+{\bm{z}}_{s}\right)-\nabla^{2}f({\bm{z}}_{s})\right\|\cdot\left\|\tilde{{\bm{z}}}_{s}-{\bm{z}}_{s}\right\|^{2}\leq L\tau\cdot\left\|\tilde{{\bm{z}}}_{s}-{\bm{z}}_{s}\right\|^{3},

which means

\frac{1}{t}\cdot\int_{0}^{t}h^{(2)}(\tau)-h^{(2)}(0)\mathrm{d}\tau\leq\frac{L\left\|\tilde{{\bm{z}}}_{s}-{\bm{z}}_{s}\right\|^{3}}{t}\cdot\int_{0}^{t}\tau\mathrm{d}\tau\leq\frac{tLr^{3}}{2}.

(81)

Combining Eq.
79
, Eq.
80
and Eq.
81
, we have

\delta^{(2)}(0)\leq\frac{2\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}r}{t}+\frac{Lr^{3}t}{2},

which means the final energy estimation error will be

\displaystyle\left|h(1)-h(0)-\left(\tilde{h}^{(1)}(0)+\frac{\tilde{h}^{(1)}(t)-\tilde{h}^{(1)}(0)}{2t}\right)\right|

(82)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\delta^{(1)}(0)}{1}+\frac{\delta^{(2)}(0)}{2}+\frac{Lr^{3}}{3!}=\underbrace{\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}\cdot r}_{\mathrm{Term\ 1}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(\frac{2\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}r}{t}+\frac{Lr^{3}t}{2}\right)}_{\mathrm{Term\ 2}}+\frac{Lr^{3}}{6}.

Considering
\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}
is extremely small (compared with the output performance error tolerance
\epsilon
), we can choose
t
depending on
\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}
, e.g.,
t=\sqrt{\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}}
, to make Term 1 and Term 2 in Eq.
82
diminish.
Under this condition, the term
Lr^{3}/6
will dominate RHS of Eq.
82
.
Besides, we have

r=3\cdot\sqrt{\tau d\log\frac{8S}{\epsilon}}=\tilde{O}(\tau^{1/2}d^{1/2}),

then we have

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\mathrm{energy}}=\mathcal{O}(Lr^{3})=\mathcal{O}(Ld^{3/2}\tau^{3/2})=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(L^{-2}d^{3/2}\left(d+m_{2}^{2}+\|\hat{{\bm{x}}}_{k}\|^{2}\right)^{-3/2}\hat{\tau}^{3/2}\right)

where the last equation follows from the choice of
\tau
shown in Theorem
C.15
.
Then, plugging this result into Theorem
C.17
and considering
\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}\ll\epsilon
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}\left(\hat{p}_{K\eta},p_{*}\right)\leq

\displaystyle\epsilon+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(Ld^{1/2}\rho^{-1}\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}})+\mathcal{O}(\hat{\tau}^{-1/2}\cdot L^{2}(d^{1/2}+m_{2}+Z){\rho^{-1}\epsilon_{\mathrm{energy}}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\hat{\tau}(d^{1/2}+m_{2}+Z)\rho^{-1}\right)

with a gradient complexity as follows

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(L^{4}\rho^{-2}\hat{\tau}^{-1}\cdot\left(d+m_{2}^{2}\right)^{2}Z^{2}\right).

Then, by choosing

\tau=\frac{\epsilon\rho}{d^{1/2}+m_{2}+Z},

we have
\mathrm{TV}\left(\hat{p}_{K\eta},p_{*}\right)\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
with

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(L^{4}\rho^{-3}\cdot\left(d+m_{2}^{2}\right)^{2}Z^{3}\cdot\epsilon\right).

Hence, the proof is completed.
∎

Remark 3
.

If we consider more high-order smooth, i.e.,

\left\|\nabla^{(u)}f({\bm{z}})\right\|\leq L,

with similar techniques shown in Corollary
C.20
, we can have the following bound, i.e.,

\epsilon_{\mathrm{energy}}=\mathcal{O}(Lr^{u})

when
\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}
is extremely small.
Under this condition, since it has

r=3\cdot\sqrt{\tau d\log\frac{8S}{\epsilon}}=\tilde{O}(\tau^{1/2}d^{1/2}),

we have

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\mathrm{energy}}=\mathcal{O}(Lr^{u})=\mathcal{O}(Ld^{u/2}\tau^{u/2})=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(L^{-u+1}d^{u/2}\left(d+m_{2}^{2}+\|\hat{{\bm{x}}}_{k}\|^{2}\right)^{-u/2}\hat{\tau}^{u/2}\right)=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(L^{-u+1}\hat{\tau}^{u/2}).

Then, plugging this result into Theorem
C.17
and considering
\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}\ll\epsilon
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}\left(\hat{p}_{K\eta},p_{*}\right)\leq

\displaystyle\epsilon+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(Ld^{1/2}\rho^{-1}\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}})+\mathcal{O}(\hat{\tau}^{-1/2}\cdot L^{2}(d^{1/2}+m_{2}+Z){\rho^{-1}\epsilon_{\mathrm{energy}}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\hat{\tau}^{(u-1)/2}L^{-u+3}(d^{1/2}+m_{2}+Z)\rho^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\hat{\tau}^{(u-1)/2}(d^{1/2}+m_{2}+Z)\rho^{-1}\right)

where we suppose
L\geq 1
in the last equation without loss of generality.
Then, by supposing

\hat{\tau}=\frac{\epsilon^{2/(u-1)}\rho}{d^{1/2}+m_{2}+Z}

we have
\mathrm{TV}\left(\hat{p}_{K\eta},p_{*}\right)\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
with

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(L^{4}\rho^{-3}\cdot\left(d+m_{2}^{2}\right)^{2}Z^{3}\cdot\epsilon^{-2/(u-1)}\cdot 2^{u}\right)

where the last
2^{u}
appears since the estimation of high-order derivatives requires an exponentially increasing call of score estimations.

Appendix D
Implement RTK inference with ULD

In this section, we consider introducing a ULD to sample from
p^{\leftarrow}_{k+1|k}({\bm{z}}|{\bm{x}}_{0})
.
To simplify the notation, we set

g({\bm{z}})\coloneqq f_{(K-k-1)\eta}({\bm{z}})+\frac{\left\|{\bm{x}}_{0}-{\bm{z}}\cdot e^{-\eta}\right\|^{2}}{2(1-e^{-2\eta})}

(83)

and consider
k
and
{\bm{x}}_{0}
to be fixed.
Besides, we set

p^{\leftarrow}({\bm{z}}|{\bm{x}}_{0})\coloneqq p^{\leftarrow}_{k+1|k}({\bm{z}}|{\bm{x}}_{0})\propto\exp(-g({\bm{z}}))

According to Corollary
B.5
and Corollary
B.3
, when we choose

\eta=\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{2L+1}{2L},

the log density
g
will be
L
-strongly log-concave and
3L
-smooth.

For the underdamped Langevin dynamics, we utilize a form similar to that shown in
[
36
]
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\hat{{\mathbf{z}}}_{t}=\hat{{\mathbf{v}}}_{t}\mathrm{d}t

(84)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\hat{{\mathbf{v}}}_{t}=-\gamma\hat{{\mathbf{v}}}_{t}\mathrm{d}t-{\bm{s}}_{{\bm{\theta}}}(\hat{{\mathbf{z}}}_{s\tau})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2\gamma}\mathrm{d}{\bm{B}}_{t}

with a little abuse of notation for
t\in[s\tau,(s+1)\tau)
.
We denote the underlying distribution of
(\hat{{\mathbf{z}}}_{t},\hat{{\mathbf{v}}}_{t})
as
\hat{\pi}_{t}
, and the exact continuous SDE

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{z}}_{t}={\mathbf{v}}_{t}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{v}}_{t}=-\gamma{\mathbf{v}}_{t}\mathrm{d}t-\nabla g({\mathbf{z}}_{t})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2\gamma}\mathrm{d}{\bm{B}}_{t}

has the underlying distribution
({\mathbf{z}}_{t},{\mathbf{v}}_{t})\sim\pi_{t}
.
The stationary distribution of the continuous version is defined as

\pi^{\leftarrow}({\bm{z}},{\bm{v}}|{\bm{x}}_{0})\propto\exp\left(-g({\bm{z}})-\frac{\left\|{\bm{v}}\right\|^{2}}{2}\right)

where the
{\bm{z}}
-marginal of
\pi^{\leftarrow}(\cdot|{\bm{x}}_{0})
is
p^{\leftarrow}(\cdot|{\bm{x}}_{0})
which is the desired target distribution of inner loops.
Therefore, by taking a small step size for the discretization and a large number of iterations, ULD will yield an approximate sample from
p^{\leftarrow}(\cdot|{\bm{x}}_{0})
.
Besides, in the analysis of ULD, we usually consider an alternate system of coordinates

(\phi,\psi)\coloneqq\mathcal{M}({\bm{z}},{\bm{v}})\coloneqq({\bm{z}},{\bm{z}}+\frac{2}{\gamma}{\bm{v}}),

their distributions of the continuous time iterates
\pi_{t}^{{\mathcal{M}}}
and the target in these alternate coordinates
\pi^{{\mathcal{M}}}
, respectively.
Besides, we need to define log-Sobolev inequality as follows

Definition 2

(Log-Sobolev Inequality)
.

The target distribution
p_{*}
satisfies the following inequality

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{p_{*}}\left[g^{2}\log g^{2}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{p_{*}}[g^{2}]\log\mathbb{E}_{p_{*}}[g^{2}]\leq 2C_{\mathrm{LSI}}\mathbb{E}_{p_{*}}\left\|\nabla g\right\|^{2}

with a constant
C_{\mathrm{LSI}}
for all smooth function
g\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\mathbb{E}_{p_{*}}[g^{2}]<\infty
.

Remark 4
.

Log-Sobolev inequality is a milder condition than strong log-concavity.
Suppose
p
satisfies
m
-strongly log-concavity, it satisfies
1/m
LSI, which is proved in Lemma
E.9
.

Definition 3

(Poincaré Inequality)
.

The target distribution
p
satisfies the following inequality

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{x}}\sim p}\left[\left\|g({\mathbf{x}})-\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{x}}\sim p}[g({\mathbf{x}})]\right\|^{2}\right]\leq C_{\mathrm{PI}}\mathbb{E}_{p}\left\|\nabla g\right\|^{2}

with a constant
C_{\mathrm{PI}}
for all smooth function
g\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\mathbb{E}_{p_{*}}[g^{2}]<\infty
.

In the following, we mainly follow the idea of proof shown in
[
36
]
, which provides the convergence of KL divergence for ULD, to control the error from the sampling subproblems.

Lemma D.1

(Proposition 14 in
[
36
]
)
.

Let
\pi_{t}^{{\mathcal{M}}}
denote the law of the continuous-time underdamped Langevin diffusion with
\gamma=c\sqrt{3L}
for
c\geq\sqrt{2}
in the
(\phi,\psi)
coordinates.
Suppose the initial distribution
\pi_{0}
has a log-Sobolev (LSI) constant (in the altered coordinates)
C_{\mathrm{LSI}}(\pi_{0}^{{\mathcal{M}}})
, then
\{\pi_{t}^{{\mathcal{M}}}\}
satisfies LSI with a constant that can be uniformly upper bounded by

C_{\mathrm{LSI}}(\pi_{t}^{{\mathcal{M}}})\leq\exp\left(-\sqrt{\frac{2L}{3}}\cdot t\right)\cdot C_{\mathrm{LSI}}(\pi_{0}^{{\mathcal{M}}})+\frac{2}{L}.

Lemma D.2

(Adapted from Proposition 1 of
[
23
]
)
.

Consider the following Lyapunov functional

{\mathcal{F}}(\pi^{\prime},\pi^{\leftarrow})\coloneqq\mathrm{KL}\left(\pi^{\prime}\big\|\pi^{\leftarrow}\right)+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\prime}}\left[\left\|\mathfrak{M}^{1/2}\nabla\log\frac{\pi^{\prime}}{\pi^{\leftarrow}}\right\|^{2}\right],\quad\mathrm{where}\quad\mathfrak{M}=\left[\begin{matrix}\frac{1}{12L}&\frac{1}{\sqrt{6L}}\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{6L}}&4\end{matrix}\right]\otimes{\bm{I}}_{d}.

For targets
\pi^{\leftarrow}\propto\exp(-g)
which are
3L
-smooth and satisfy LSI with constant
1/L
, let
\gamma=2\sqrt{6L}
.
Then the law
\pi_{t}
of ULD satisfies

\partial_{t}{\mathcal{F}}(\pi_{t},\pi^{\leftarrow})\leq-\frac{\sqrt{L}}{10\sqrt{6}}\cdot{\mathcal{F}}(\pi_{t},\pi^{\leftarrow}).

Lemma D.3

(Variant of Lemma 4.8 in
[
1
]
)
.

Let
\hat{\pi}_{t}
denote the law of SDE.
84
and
\pi_{t}
denote the law of the continuous time underdamped Langevin diffusion with the same initialization, i.e.,
\hat{\pi}_{0}=\pi_{0}
.
If
\gamma\asymp\sqrt{L}
and the step size
\tau
satisfies

\tau=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(L^{-3/2}d^{-1/2}T^{-1/2}\right)

then we have

\chi^{2}(\hat{\pi}_{T}\|\pi_{T})\lesssim L^{3/2}d\tau^{2}T+\epsilon^{2}_{\mathrm{score}}L^{-1/2}T

Proof.

The main difference of this discretization analysis is whether the score
\nabla\log p_{t}
can be exactly obtained or only be approximated by
{\bm{s}}_{{\bm{\theta}}}
.
Therefore, in this proof, we will omit various steps the same as those shown in
[
1
]
.

We consider the following difference

\displaystyle G_{T}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\gamma}}\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}\int_{s\tau}^{(s+1)\tau}\left<\nabla g({\bm{z}}_{t})-{\bm{s}}_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\bm{z}}_{s\tau}),\mathrm{d}{\bm{B}}_{t}\right>

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4\gamma}\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}\int_{s\tau}^{(s+1)\tau}\left\|\nabla g({\bm{z}}_{t})-{\bm{s}}_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\bm{z}}_{s\tau})\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}t.

From Girsanov’s theorem, we obtain immediately using Itô’s formula

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{T}}\left[\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\hat{\pi}_{T}}{\mathrm{d}\pi_{T}}\right)^{2}\right]-1=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(2G_{T}\right)\right]-1=\frac{1}{2\gamma}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{T}}\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}\left[\int_{s\tau}^{(s+1)\tau}\exp(2G_{t})\left\|\nabla g({\bm{z}}_{t})-{\bm{s}}_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\bm{z}}_{s\tau})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\gamma}\cdot\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}\int_{s\tau}^{(s+1)\tau}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\exp(4G_{t})\right]\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla g({\bm{z}}_{t})-{\bm{s}}_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\bm{z}}_{s\tau})\right\|^{4}\right]}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{4}{\gamma}\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}\cdot\int_{s\tau}^{(s+1)\tau}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\exp(4G_{t})\right]\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla g({\bm{z}}_{t})-\nabla g({\bm{z}}_{s\tau})\right\|^{4}\right]}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+\frac{4\epsilon^{2}_{\mathrm{score}}}{\gamma}\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}\int_{s\tau}^{(s+1)\tau}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\exp(4G_{t})\right]}\mathrm{d}t

According to Corollary 20 of
[
36
]
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\exp(4G_{t})\right]\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{16}{\gamma}\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}\int_{s\tau}^{(s+1)\tau\wedge t}\left\|\nabla g({\bm{z}}_{r})-{\bm{s}}_{{\bm{\theta}}}({\bm{z}}_{s\tau})\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}r\right)\right]}

(85)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\exp\left[\frac{32}{\gamma}\cdot\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}\left(\int_{s\tau}^{(s+1)\tau\wedge t}\left\|\nabla g({\bm{z}}_{r})-\nabla g({\bm{z}}_{s\tau})\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}r+\int_{s\tau}^{(s+1)\tau\wedge t}\epsilon^{2}_{\mathrm{score}}\mathrm{d}r\right)\right]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{16t\epsilon^{2}_{\mathrm{score}}}{\gamma}\right)\cdot\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\exp\left[\frac{32}{\gamma}\cdot\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}\int_{s\tau}^{(s+1)\tau\wedge t}\left\|\nabla g({\bm{z}}_{r})-\nabla g({\bm{z}}_{s\tau})\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}r\right]}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 3\cdot\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\exp\left[\frac{32}{\gamma}\cdot\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}\int_{s\tau}^{(s+1)\tau\wedge t}\left\|\nabla g({\bm{z}}_{r})-\nabla g({\bm{z}}_{s\tau})\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}r\right]},

where the last inequality can be established by requiring

\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}=\mathcal{O}\left(\gamma^{1/2}T^{-1/2}\right)\quad\Rightarrow\quad\frac{16t\epsilon^{2}_{\mathrm{score}}}{\gamma}\leq 1

since
\exp(u)\leq 1+2u
for any
u\in[0,1]
.

With similar techniques utilized in Lemma 4.8 of
[
1
]
, we know that if

\gamma\asymp\sqrt{3L},\quad\tau\lesssim\frac{\gamma^{1/2}}{6L\cdot d^{1/3}T^{1/2}(\log S)^{1/2}},\quad\mathrm{and}\quad T\gtrsim\frac{\sqrt{3L}}{L}=\sqrt{\frac{3}{L}},

it holds that

\mathbb{E}\exp\left[\frac{32}{\gamma}\cdot\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}\int_{s\tau}^{(s+1)\tau\wedge t}\left\|\nabla g({\bm{z}}_{r})-\nabla g({\bm{z}}_{s\tau})\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}r\right]\leq\exp\left(\mathcal{O}\left(L^{3/2}d\tau^{2}T\log S\right)\right).

Furthermore, for

\tau\lesssim L^{-3/2}d^{-1/2}T^{-1/2}(\log S)^{-1/2},

it has

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\ \mathbb{E}\left[\exp(4G_{t})\right]\lesssim 1.

Then, still with similar techniques utilized in Lemma 4.8 of
[
1
]
, we have

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla g({\bm{z}}_{t})-\nabla g({\bm{z}}_{s\tau})\right\|^{4}\right]}\leq(3L)^{2}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|{\bm{z}}_{t}-{\bm{z}}_{s\tau}\right\|^{4}\right]}\lesssim L^{2}d\tau^{2}.

In summary, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{T}}\left[\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\hat{\pi}_{T}}{\mathrm{d}\pi_{T}}\right)^{2}\right]-1\lesssim\frac{L^{2}d\tau^{2}T}{\gamma}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}_{\mathrm{score}}T}{\gamma},

and the proof is completed.
∎

Corollary D.4
.

Under the same assumptions and hyperparameter settings made in Lemma
D.3
.
If the step size
\tau
and the score estimation error
\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}
satisfies

\tau=\tilde{\Theta}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{L^{3/4}d^{1/2}T^{1/2}}\right)\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}=\mathcal{O}\left(T^{-1/2}\epsilon\right)

Then we have
\chi^{2}(\hat{\pi}_{T}\|\pi_{T})\lesssim\epsilon^{2}
.

Proof.

We can easily obtain this result by plugging the choice of
\tau
and
\epsilon
into Lemma
D.3
. Noted that we suppose
L\geq 1
without loss of generality.
∎

Theorem D.5

(Variant of Theorem 6 in
[
36
]
)
.

Under Assumption
[A1]
–
[A2]
, for any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, we require Gaussian-type initialization and high-accurate score estimation, i.e.,

\hat{\pi}_{0}=\mathcal{N}({\bm{0}},e^{2\eta}-1)\otimes\mathcal{N}({\bm{0}},{\bm{I}})\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon).

If we set the step size and the iteration number as

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle=\tilde{\Theta}\left(\epsilon d^{-1/2}L^{-1/2}\cdot\left(\log\left[\frac{L(d+m_{2}^{2}+\|{\bm{x}}_{0}\|^{2})}{\epsilon^{2}}\right]\right)^{-1/2}\right)

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=\tilde{\Theta}\left(\epsilon^{-1}d^{1/2}\cdot\left(\log\left[\frac{L(d+m_{2}^{2}+\|{\bm{x}}_{0}\|^{2})}{\epsilon^{2}}\right]\right)^{1/2}\right).

the marginal distribution of output particles
\hat{p}_{T}
will satisfy
\mathrm{KL}\left(\hat{p}_{T}\big\|p^{\leftarrow}(\cdot|{\bm{x}}_{0})\right)\leq\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})
.

Proof.

Consider the underlying distribution of the twisted coordinates
(\phi,\psi)
for SDE.
84
, the decomposition of the KL using Cauchy–Schwarz:

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\left(\hat{\pi}_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}\big\|\pi^{{\mathcal{M}}}\right)=

\displaystyle\int\log\frac{\hat{\pi}_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}}{\pi^{{\mathcal{M}}}}\mathrm{d}\hat{\pi}_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}=\mathrm{KL}\left(\hat{\pi}_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}\big\|\pi_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}\right)+\int\log\frac{\pi_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}}{\pi^{{\mathcal{M}}}}\mathrm{d}\hat{\pi}_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}

(86)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\left(\hat{\pi}_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}\big\|\pi_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}\right)+\mathrm{KL}\left(\pi_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}\big\|\pi^{{\mathcal{M}}}\right)+\int\log\frac{\pi_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}}{\pi^{{\mathcal{M}}}}\mathrm{d}(\hat{\pi}_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}-\pi_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\left(\hat{\pi}_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}\big\|\pi_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}\right)+\mathrm{KL}\left(\pi_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}\big\|\pi^{{\mathcal{M}}}\right)+\sqrt{\chi^{2}\left(\hat{\pi}_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}\|\pi_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}\right)\times\mathrm{var}_{\pi_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}}\left(\log\frac{\pi_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}}{\pi^{{\mathcal{M}}}}\right)}.

Using LSI of the iterations via Lemma
D.1
, we have

\mathrm{var}_{\pi_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}}\left(\log\frac{\pi_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}}{\pi^{{\mathcal{M}}}}\right)\leq C_{\mathrm{LSI}}(\pi_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}})\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}}\left[\left\|\nabla\log\frac{\pi_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}}{\pi^{{\mathcal{M}}}}\right\|^{2}\right]\lesssim\frac{1}{L}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}}\left[\left\|\nabla\log\frac{\pi_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}}{\pi^{{\mathcal{M}}}}\right\|^{2}\right].

Then, we start to upper bound the relative Fisher information.
Since
\pi^{{\mathcal{M}}}={\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\pi^{\leftarrow}(\cdot|{\bm{x}}_{0})
, then

\pi^{{\mathcal{M}}}(\phi,\psi)\propto\pi^{\leftarrow}({\mathcal{M}}^{-1}(\phi,\psi)|{\bm{x}}_{0}).

Therefore, we have

\nabla\log\pi^{{\mathcal{M}}}=({\mathcal{M}}^{-1})^{\top}\nabla\log\pi^{\leftarrow}(\cdot|{\bm{x}}_{0})\circ{\mathcal{M}}^{-1},

and similarly for
\nabla\log\pi_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}
.
This yields the expression

\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}}\left[\left\|\nabla\log\frac{\pi_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}}{\pi^{{\mathcal{M}}}}\right\|^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{T}}\left[\left\|({\mathcal{M}}^{-1})^{\top}\nabla\log\frac{\pi_{T}}{\pi^{\leftarrow}}\right\|^{2}\right].

(87)

According to the definition of
{\mathcal{M}}
, we have

{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}({\mathcal{M}}^{-1})^{\top}=\left[\begin{matrix}1&-\gamma/2\\
-\gamma/2&\gamma^{2}/2\end{matrix}\right].

For any
c_{0}>0
and

\mathfrak{M}\coloneqq\left[\begin{matrix}\frac{1}{12L}&\frac{1}{\sqrt{6L}}\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{6L}}&4\end{matrix}\right]\otimes{\bm{I}}_{d},

we have

L\mathfrak{M}-c_{0}{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}({\mathcal{M}}^{-1})^{\top}=\left[\begin{matrix}1/4-c_{0}&\sqrt{3L}(1/\sqrt{2}+c_{0}\sqrt{2})\\
\sqrt{3L}(1/\sqrt{2}+c_{0}\sqrt{2})&3L(4-c_{0})\end{matrix}\right].

The determinant is

3L\cdot\left[\left(\frac{1}{4}-c_{0}\right)\cdot(4-c_{0})-\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}+c_{0}\sqrt{2}\right)^{2}\right]>0

for
c_{0}>0
sufficiently small, which means that

{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}({\mathcal{M}}^{-1})^{\top}\preceq c_{0}^{-1}L\mathfrak{M}.

Therefore, Eq.
87
becomes

\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}}\left[\left\|\nabla\log\frac{\pi_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}}{\pi^{{\mathcal{M}}}}\right\|^{2}\right]\lesssim 3L\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{T}}\left[\left\|\mathfrak{M}^{1/2}\nabla\log\frac{\pi_{T}}{\pi^{\leftarrow}}\right\|^{2}\right].

According to Lemma
D.2
, the decay of the Fisher information requires us to set

T\gtrsim L^{-1/2}\cdot\log\left[\epsilon^{-2}\cdot\left(\mathrm{KL}\left(\pi_{0}\big\|\pi^{\leftarrow}\right)+\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}\left(\left\|\mathfrak{M}^{1/2}\nabla\log\frac{\pi_{0}}{\pi^{\leftarrow}}\right\|^{2}\right)\right)\right],

(88)

which yields
\mathrm{KL}\left(\pi_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}\big\|\pi^{{\mathcal{M}}}\right)\leq\epsilon^{2}
.
Besides, we can easily have

\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}\left(\left\|\mathfrak{M}^{1/2}\nabla\log\frac{\pi_{0}}{\pi^{\leftarrow}}\right\|^{2}\right)\lesssim\frac{1}{3L}\cdot\mathrm{FI}\left(\pi_{0}\big\|\pi^{\leftarrow}\right)=\frac{1}{3L}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}\left(\left\|\nabla\log\frac{\pi_{0}}{\pi^{\leftarrow}}\right\|^{2}\right).

According to the definition of LSI, we also have

\mathrm{KL}\left(\pi_{0}\big\|\pi^{\leftarrow}\right)\leq\frac{C_{\mathrm{LSI}}}{2}\cdot\mathrm{FI}\left(\pi_{0}\big\|\pi^{\leftarrow}\right)=\frac{1}{2L}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}\left(\left\|\nabla\log\frac{\pi_{0}}{\pi^{\leftarrow}}\right\|^{2}\right).

Recall as well that this requires
\gamma\asymp\sqrt{3L}
in SDE.
84
.
For the remaining
\mathrm{KL}\left(\hat{\pi}_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}\big\|\pi_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}\right)
and
\chi^{2}\left(\hat{\pi}_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}\|\pi_{T}^{{\mathcal{M}}}\right)
in Eq.
86
, we invoke Lemma
D.3
with the value
T=S\tau
specified and desired accuracy
\epsilon
, , which consequently yields

\tau=\tilde{\Theta}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{L^{3/4}d^{1/2}T^{1/2}}\right)\quad\mathrm{and}\quad S=\tilde{\Theta}\left(\frac{T^{3/2}L^{3/4}d^{1/2}}{\epsilon}\right).

(89)

Under this condition, we start to consider the initialization error.
Suppose we have
\pi_{0}=\mathcal{N}({\bm{0}},e^{2\eta}-1)\otimes\mathcal{N}({\bm{0}},{\bm{I}})
, which implies

\displaystyle\mathrm{FI}\left(\pi_{0}\big\|\pi^{\leftarrow}\right)\lesssim

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}\left[\left\|\nabla f_{(K-k-1)\eta}({\mathbf{z}})-\nabla f_{(K-k-1)\eta}({\bm{0}})+\nabla f_{(K-k-1)\eta}({\bm{0}})-\frac{e^{-\eta}{\bm{x}}_{0}}{1-e^{-2\eta}}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 3L^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}[\|{\mathbf{z}}\|^{2}]+3\left\|\nabla f_{(K-k-1)\eta}({\bm{0}})\right\|^{2}+\frac{3e^{-2\eta}}{(1-e^{-2\eta})^{2}}\cdot\left\|{\bm{x}}_{0}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3L^{2}\cdot\left(e^{2\eta}-1\right)+3\left\|\nabla f_{(K-k-1)\eta}({\bm{0}})\right\|^{2}+\frac{3e^{-2\eta}}{(1-e^{-2\eta})^{2}}\cdot\left\|{\bm{x}}_{0}\right\|^{2}

Following the
\eta
setting, i.e.,

\eta=\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{2L+1}{2L}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad e^{2\eta}=\frac{2L+1}{2L},

which yields

\displaystyle\mathrm{FI}\left(\pi_{0}\big\|\pi^{\leftarrow}\right)\lesssim

\displaystyle L+\left\|\nabla f_{(K-k-1)\eta}({\bm{0}})\right\|^{2}+L^{2}\|{\bm{x}}_{0}\|^{2}

(90)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle L+L^{2}(d+m_{2}^{2})+L^{2}\|{\bm{x}}_{0}\|^{2}

where the inequality follows from Eq.
72
.
Therefore, combining Eq.
90
, Eq.
89
and Eq.
88
, we have

T^{1/2}\gtrsim L^{-1/4}\cdot\left(\log\left[\frac{L(d+m_{2}^{2}+\|{\bm{x}}_{0}\|^{2})}{\epsilon^{2}}\right]\right)^{1/2}\gtrsim L^{1/4}\cdot\left(\log\left[\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0}}\left(\left\|\nabla\log\frac{\pi_{0}}{\pi^{\leftarrow}}\right\|^{2}\right)}{L\epsilon^{2}}\right]\right)^{1/2},

which implies

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle=\tilde{\Theta}\left(\epsilon d^{-1/2}L^{-1/2}\cdot\left(\log\left[\frac{L(d+m_{2}^{2}+\|{\bm{x}}_{0}\|^{2})}{\epsilon^{2}}\right]\right)^{-1/2}\right)

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=\tilde{\Theta}\left(\epsilon^{-1}d^{1/2}\cdot\left(\log\left[\frac{L(d+m_{2}^{2}+\|{\bm{x}}_{0}\|^{2})}{\epsilon^{2}}\right]\right)^{1/2}\right).

In this condition, the score estimation error is required to be

\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}=\mathcal{O}\left(\gamma^{1/2}T^{-1/2}\cdot\epsilon\right)=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\epsilon/\sqrt{L}\right).

Hence, the proof is completed.
∎

Theorem D.6
.

Under Assumption
[A1]
–
[A2]
, for Alg.
1
, we choose

\eta=\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{2L+1}{2L}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad K=4L\cdot\log\frac{(1+L^{2})d+\left\|\nabla f_{*}({\bm{0}})\right\|^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}

and implement Step 3 of Alg.
1
with projected Alg.
3
.
For the
k
-th run of Alg.
3
, we require Gaussian-type initialization and high-accurate score estimation, i.e.,

\hat{\pi}_{0}=\mathcal{N}({\bm{0}},e^{2\eta}-1)\otimes\mathcal{N}({\bm{0}},{\bm{I}})\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon).

If we set the hyperparameters as shown in Lemma
D.5
, it can achieve
\mathrm{TV}\left(\hat{p}_{K\eta},p_{*}\right)\lesssim\epsilon

with an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(L^{2}d^{1/2}\epsilon^{-1}\right)
gradient complexity.

Proof.

According to Corollary
B.5
, we know that under the choice

\eta=\frac{1}{2}\ln\frac{2L+1}{2L},

it requires to run Alg.
3
for
K
times where

K=4L\cdot\log\frac{(1+L^{2})d+\left\|\nabla f_{*}({\bm{0}})\right\|^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}.

For each run of Alg.
3
, we require the KL divergence error to achieve

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\left(\hat{p}_{(k+1)\eta|k\eta}(\cdot|\hat{{\bm{x}}})\big\|p^{\leftarrow}_{(k+1)\eta|k\eta}(\cdot|\hat{{\bm{x}}})\right)\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{4L}\cdot\left[\log\frac{(1+L^{2})d+\left\|\nabla f_{*}({\bm{0}})\right\|^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}\right]^{-1}.

Combining with Theorem
D.5
, we consider a step size

\displaystyle\tau_{k}=

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(L^{-1}d^{-1/2}\epsilon\cdot(\log\left[L^{2}\cdot(d+m_{2}^{2}+\|\hat{{\bm{x}}}_{k}\|^{2})\right])^{-1/2}\right)

then the iteration number will be

S_{k}=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(L^{1/2}d^{1/2}\epsilon^{-1}\cdot(\log\left[L^{2}\cdot(d+m_{2}^{2}+\|\hat{{\bm{x}}}_{k}\|^{2})\right])^{1/2}\right).

For an expectation perspective, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\hat{p}_{k\eta}}\left[\log(L^{2}\|\hat{{\mathbf{x}}}_{k}\|^{2})\right]\leq\log\left[\mathbb{E}_{\hat{p}_{k\eta}}(\|\hat{{\mathbf{x}}}_{k}\|^{2})\right]=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(L)

where the last inequality follows from Lemma
B.6
.
This means that with the total gradient complexity

\displaystyle K\cdot S=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(L^{2}d^{1/2}\epsilon^{-1}\right)

Hence, the proof is completed.
∎

Appendix E
Auxiliary Lemmas

Lemma E.1

(Theorem 4 in
[
32
]
)
.

Suppose
p\propto\exp(-f)
defined on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfies LSI with constant
\mu>0
.
Along the Langevin dynamics, i.e.,

\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{x}}_{t}=-\nabla f({\mathbf{x}})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}{\bm{B}}_{t},

where
{\mathbf{x}}_{t}\sim p_{t}
, then it has

\mathrm{KL}\left(p_{t}\big\|p\right)\leq\exp\left(-2\mu t\right)\cdot\mathrm{KL}\left(p_{0}\big\|p\right).

Lemma E.2
.

Suppose
p\propto\exp(-f)
defined on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfies LSI with constant
\mu>0
where
f
is L-smooth, i.e.,

\left\|\nabla f({\bm{x}}^{\prime})-\nabla f({\bm{x}})\right\|\leq L\left\|{\bm{x}}^{\prime}-{\bm{x}}\right\|.

If
p_{0}
is the standard Gaussian distribution defined on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then we have

\mathrm{KL}\left(p_{0}\big\|p\right)\leq\frac{(1+2L^{2})d+2\left\|\nabla f({\bm{0}})\right\|^{2}}{\mu}.

Proof.

According to the definition of LSI, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\left(p_{0}\big\|p\right)\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\mu}\int p_{0}({\bm{x}})\left\|\nabla\log\frac{p_{0}({\bm{x}})}{p({\bm{x}})}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}=\frac{1}{2\mu}\int p_{0}({\bm{x}})\left\|-{\bm{x}}+\nabla f({\bm{x}})\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mu^{-1}\cdot\left[\int p_{0}({\bm{x}})\|{\bm{x}}\|^{2}\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}+\int p_{0}({\bm{x}})\|\nabla f({\bm{x}})-\nabla f({\bm{0}})+\nabla f({\bm{0}})\|^{2}\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mu^{-1}\cdot\left[(1+2L^{2})\int p_{0}({\bm{x}})\|{\bm{x}}\|^{2}\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}+2\left\|\nabla f({\bm{0}})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(1+2L^{2})d+2\left\|\nabla f({\bm{0}})\right\|^{2}}{\mu}

where the third inequality follows from the
L
-smoothness of
f_{*}
and the last equation establishes since
\mathbb{E}_{p_{0}}[\|{\bm{x}}\|^{2}]=d
is for the standard Gaussian distribution
p_{0}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
∎

Lemma E.3

(Variant of Lemma B.1 in
[
37
]
)
.

Suppose
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a
m
-strongly convex function and satisfies
L
-smooth. Then, we have

\nabla f({\bm{x}})\cdot{\bm{x}}\geq\frac{m\left\|{\bm{x}}\right\|^{2}}{2}-\frac{\|\nabla f({\bm{0}})\|^{2}}{2m}

where
{\bm{x}}_{*}
is the global optimum of the function
f
.

Proof.

According to the definition of strongly convex, the function
f
satisfies

\displaystyle f({\bm{0}})-f({\bm{x}})\geq\nabla f({\bm{x}})\cdot\left({\bm{0}}-{\bm{x}}\right)+\frac{m}{2}\cdot\left\|{\bm{x}}\right\|^{2}\ \Leftrightarrow\ \nabla f({\bm{x}})\cdot{\bm{x}}\geq f({\bm{x}})-f({\bm{0}})+\frac{m}{2}\cdot\left\|{\bm{x}}\right\|^{2}.

Besides, we have

f({\bm{x}})-f({\bm{0}})\geq\nabla f({\bm{0}})\cdot{\bm{x}}+\frac{m}{2}\cdot\|{\bm{x}}\|^{2}\geq\frac{m}{2}\cdot\|{\bm{x}}\|^{2}-\frac{m}{2}\cdot\|{\bm{x}}\|^{2}-\frac{\|\nabla f({\bm{0}})\|^{2}}{2m}=-\frac{\|\nabla f({\bm{0}})\|^{2}}{2m}.

Combining the above two inequalities, the proof is completed.
∎

Lemma E.4

(Lemma A.1 in
[
37
]
)
.

Suppose a function
f
satisfy

\nabla f({\bm{x}})\cdot{\bm{x}}\geq\frac{m\|{\bm{x}}\|^{2}}{2}-\frac{\|\nabla f({\bm{0}})\|}{2m},

then we have

f({\bm{x}})\geq\frac{m}{8}\|{\bm{x}}\|^{2}+f({\bm{x}}_{*})-\frac{\|\nabla f({\bm{0}})\|^{2}}{4m}.

Lemma E.5

(Lemma 1 in
[
16
]
)
.

Consider the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck forward process

\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{x}}_{t}=-{\mathbf{x}}_{t}\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}{\bm{B}}_{t},

and denote the underlying distribution of the particle
{\mathbf{x}}_{t}
as
p_{t}
.
Then, the score function can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}\ln p_{t}({\bm{x}})=\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{x}}_{0}\sim q_{t}(\cdot|{\bm{x}})}\frac{e^{-t}{\bm{x}}_{0}-{\bm{x}}}{\left(1-e^{-2t}\right)},

(91)

\displaystyle q_{t}({\bm{x}}_{0}|{\bm{x}})\propto\exp\left(-f_{*}({\bm{x}}_{0})-\frac{\left\|{\bm{x}}-e^{-t}{\bm{x}}_{0}\right\|^{2}}{2\left(1-e^{-2t}\right)}\right).

Lemma E.7

(Lemma 10 in
[
9
]
)
.

Suppose that Assumption
[A1]
–
[A2]
hold.
Let
\{{\mathbf{x}}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
denote the forward process, i.e., Eq.
1
, for all
t\geq 0
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|{\mathbf{x}}\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\max\left\{d,m_{2}^{2}\right\}.

Lemma E.8
.

Suppose
q
is a distribution which satisfies LSI with constant
\mu
, then its variance satisfies

\int q({\bm{x}})\left\|{\bm{x}}-\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{q}}\left[{\mathbf{x}}\right]\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}\leq\frac{d}{\mu}.

Proof.

It is known that LSI implies Poincaré inequality with the same constant, i.e.,
\mu
, which means if for all smooth
function
g\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,

\mathrm{var}_{q}\left(g({\mathbf{x}})\right)\leq\frac{1}{\mu}\mathbb{E}_{q}\left[\left\|\nabla g({\mathbf{x}})\right\|^{2}\right].

In this condition, we suppose
{\bm{b}}=\mathbb{E}_{q}[{\mathbf{x}}]
, and have the following equation

\displaystyle\int q({\bm{x}})\left\|{\bm{x}}-\mathbb{E}_{q}\left[{\mathbf{x}}\right]\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}=\int q({\bm{x}})\left\|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{b}}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\sum_{i=1}^{d}q({\bm{x}})\left({\bm{x}}_{i}-{\bm{b}}_{i}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int q({\bm{x}})\left(\left<{\bm{x}},{\bm{e}}_{i}\right>-\left<{\bm{b}},{\bm{e}}_{i}\right>\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int q({\bm{x}})\left(\left<{\bm{x}},{\bm{e}}_{i}\right>-\mathbb{E}_{q}\left[\left<{\mathbf{x}},{\bm{e}}_{i}\right>\right]\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathrm{var}_{q}\left(g_{i}({\mathbf{x}})\right)

where
g_{i}({\bm{x}})
is defined as
g_{i}({\bm{x}})\coloneqq\left<{\bm{x}},{\bm{e}}_{i}\right>

and
{\bm{e}}_{i}
is a one-hot vector ( the
i
-th element of
{\bm{e}}_{i}
is
1
others are
0
).
Combining this equation and Poincaré inequality, for each
i
, we have

\mathrm{var}_{q}\left(g_{i}({\mathbf{x}})\right)\leq\frac{1}{\mu}\mathbb{E}_{q}\left[\left\|{\bm{e}}_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]=\frac{1}{\mu}.

Hence, the proof is completed.
∎

Lemma E.9

(Variant of Lemma 10 in
[
11
]
)
.

Suppose
-\log p_{*}
is
m
-strongly convex function, for any distribution with density function
p
, we have

\mathrm{KL}\left(p\big\|p_{*}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2m}\int p({\bm{x}})\left\|\nabla\log\frac{p({\bm{x}})}{p_{*}({\bm{x}})}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}.

By choosing
p({\bm{x}})=g^{2}({\bm{x}})p_{*}({\bm{x}})/\mathbb{E}_{p_{*}}\left[g^{2}({\mathbf{x}})\right]
for the test function
g\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{E}_{p_{*}}\left[g^{2}({\mathbf{x}})\right]<\infty
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{p_{*}}\left[g^{2}\log g^{2}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{p_{*}}\left[g^{2}\right]\log\mathbb{E}_{p_{*}}\left[g^{2}\right]\leq\frac{2}{m}\mathbb{E}_{p_{*}}\left[\left\|\nabla g\right\|^{2}\right],

which implies
p_{*}
satisfies
1/m
-log-Sobolev inequality.
